=== Page 244 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
discreet inquiries that have been made of trustworthy
and reliable sources here have not elicited any in-
formation in the matter.
The Consul General, Mr. Leon Siguenza, the person
in question, has, however, since the date of the
Department's instruction, been recalled by his Govern-
ment. A notice in this regard appeared in the Official
Gazette of June 20, 1941, stating, in translation,
that "notification has been received from the Foreign
Minister of El Salvador, through a letter dated May 6,
194l, to the effect that Leon Siguenza, Consul General
of that country at Tokyo, has been granted resignation
as of May 6, 194l". From the reports that have been
heard here, it does not seem likely that resignation
was at the volition of Mr. Siguenza and it is believed
that he has been discharged from his duties. As re-
ported in the Embassy's telegram No. 1ll6, July 29,
4 p.m., Mr. Siguenza has made application at this
Embassy for an American diplomatic vis& in order to
proceed to the United States. When questioned as to
his present status he gave a vague and evasive answer.
He stated that he proposed to depart for the United
States on or about August 21.
It will be recalled, as reported in the Embassy's
despatch No. 8ll, June 1, 1934, that Mr. Siguenza was
the representative of the Salvadoran Government at
Tokyo in 1934 at the time that El Salvador recognized
"Manohukuo" and that itwas he who transmitted the
communioation to the "Manchukuo" Minister at Tokyo
extending

=== Page 245 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
extending formal recognition. What if any other con-
nection he might have had in that matter was not then
disclosed.
Mr. Siguenza has been the representative here of
El Salvador for some fourteen years. He is a bachelor
and has fairly wide acquaintances in the foreign and
Japanese communities. His habits could hardly be called
regular and he has been addicted to heavy drinking. It
is also a matter of common knowledge that he has an
illegitimate daughter by a Japanese mistress. To sum
up, it can scarcely be said that his reputation is a
savory one.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

WAR DEPARTMENT
M. I. D.
Angust 20, 1941
(Date)
SubjOCT: AXIS COMMUNICATION WITH SOUTH AMERICA BY
MEANS OF FILM CANS
Summary of Information:
1. It is reported to this office by a reliable source that secret
channels of communication have been established between South America
and Japan by the following method:
Messages are placed in cans of motion picture film in certain
cities in South America and are shipped via the Panama Canal
or the West coast to Japan.
These cans are marked by crayon and otherwise to indicate
which ones contain messages. Upon arrival in Japan, all
shipments of film cans are examined and the messages are
picked up by agents of the Japanese military or naval in-
telligence.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
VISION OF THE AMERICAN RERUBLICS
MacLean:
The group of Japanese listed on the at-
tached flimsy, beginning with Nakamura and
concluding with Sekidera, have been moving
about in the western hemisphere for a con-
siderable period. It is my impression that
Nakamura is the most important member of the
group. I do not know exactly what they are
up to. There have been numerous reports---
as I recall them, of a vague nature. It
appears to me that they should be watched.
Do you concur in asking FC to attempt to
collect what we have about these individuals
in the files and to request Mexico City to
inform the Department about their activities
in Mexico. I knote that all but two of them
appear not to have been carded. Nevertheless,
it may be possible to get what we have from
DCR. I should think that most of it would
be entered in the 894. file or cross-referenced.
If you concur, please so indicate and
pass on.

My dear Mr. Berle:
As of pos. ible interest to you, information has been received
from a confidential source believed to be reliable, that the Japanese
have an organization in South America with reference to Japanese intra-
South American trade. This organization is reported by the informant to
O
have been created during the conference of Japanese traders at Rio de
Janeiro.
According to the informant, South America was divided into
two separate zones by the Japanese, the first one extending from Panama
to Mexico, allotted to the Mitsui firm, and the other embracing all of
South America, and under the control of the Mitsubishi firm. The ob-
ject of operating in this fashion, according to the informant, is to
P
save expenses.
S
Informant stated that the Mitsubishi firm in Tokyo sends cabled
instructions to Buenos Aires and Sao Paulo, and a word in cipher is addedT
to the cable to indicate which country the cable belongs to, and to which
country the cable has to be transmitted. The codes used are generally
in the Japanese language.
The following list of articles was established at the confer-
ence in Rio de Janeiro as the articles for the Japanese intra-South
Confidential File
American trade:
1. Argentine cement for Chile and Brazil
2. Venezuelan coffee for all South American countries
Dvision of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
3. Brazilian oranges for all South American countries,
from "Casa Tozan"
1941
4. Brazilian textiles for all South American countries
from "Casa Tozan"
Department of State
5. Brazilian pig iron for Argentina
6. Brazilian koolin (porcelain clay) and wax for all
PILED
South American countries.

JAPANESE PROPAGANDA AND ESPIONAGE IN NORTH AND SOUTH AMERICA
WITH LICT OF POSSIBLE PROPAGANDA AGENTS AND SPIES
SUMMARY OF INFCHMATION IN THE FILES OF THE COI
I.

distribution of Japanese nationals possibly engaged in propaganda and
espionage.
Cont Secrotary
tato
A. United St:tes.
1.1941
1. Document No. 1019 C. Report from ONI, May, 1941,
MR. ACHESON
"Totalitarian Doctrine, Organization, Propaganda
Distribution, and Intelligence Operations - Japanism."
An elaborate analysis (15 pp.) of aims, methods, and
prticipating groups; no list, however, of specific persons
engaged in Japanese propaganda in the U. S.
K3
2.
Document No. 2297 S. Two-page unsigned memorandum,
"Activities of Japanese Consulate General in New York."
Personnel of Consulate General in N. Y., and of the
Japanese Embassy in Washington, and the rivalry between
them.
3.
Document No. 5265 C. Report from F.B.I., "Memorandum,
Re: Japanese Espionage," 11/17/41.
A most valuable source of information (29 pp.) about
the organization and accomplishments of Japanese espionage
in the U. S., with names of agents and messages despatched
to Japan.
B. Central and South America
1. Document No. 1816 C. Report prepared by G-2, War Department,
(10/16/41)"Distribution and Activities of Japanese in Latin America."
Confidential File
An elaborate analysis (34 pp. with maps) of the distri-
bution and activity of Japanese in each Latin American coun-
try, compiled for the most part in early October, 1941.
Does not, however, list names of individual Japanese, nor
does it class "propagandist" as an occupation.

meseages from Tokyo to La Pas, Bolivia were noted by
our radio monitor. These messages were for relay to

Mr. Bonsa1:
Thi telegram, indicating a remarkable amount of
message traffice ton Santiago, is interesting as a twin
to the one I snowed you earlier indicating a large
amount (via La Paz) to Lima. It is interesting that
Tokio has communicated in the last days direct with
La Paz for Lima, but via Germany for Santiago.
The two points receiving the most telegrams are on
the west coast. The principle Japunese colony on the
Berlin, but German messages also.
FW 894.20210/127
We have had excellent evidence (the best possible)
that German and Japanese espionage services have been
collaborating closely in the other American republics,
at least since October. The events of the last week
in the Pacific bear the earmarks of close German-Japanese
collaboration.
cand
Frankly, I do not know what to expect, but I begin
to expect something in Chile and Peru. I believe that
these two countries should be very discreetly warned
that this government feels something may be brewing in
the way of collaboration between their colonies of
Germans and Japanese and the naval forces of the As.
Specifically, if I had to guess, I should say we shoul
look out for raids intended to accomplish demoliti?
and to distract the forces of this government, as wall
s
to make an impression on public opinion. Some sortoof
Axis naval showing, in the South Atlantic or in the south
Pacific, to underscore our naval defeat, is in the books.
Whereas Peru, with its large Japanese colony, gets
instructions from Tokio; Chile, with its German colony,
gets German-Japanese as well as German instructions.
Why do the Japanese in Chile, where there are few Japanese
get instructions, as well as the Germans? Does it not
ential File
mean that Japanese action --- necessarily naval --- is
to be expected in both countries?
Toop.
PS/HM
RA:JRT

About 30 messages in Japanese Government code, all
signed Togo, were radioed from Tokyo December lo to a

27
thence to Lima under signature Iriyerbo. Heretofore only

and Buenos Aires to Santiago. An unusually large number
PS/HMConfidentia
of messages in German Government code, emanating from

changed routing may indicate activity on the part of German
and Japanese elements in Chile and Peru which would call
for increased vigilance on the part of the local authorities.

authorities in accordance with existing international

country desk officers to review

1on whether there is in faot any

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
894.20210/143
Sir:
With reference to the Department's circular tele-
gram of January 6, 4 a.m. and my telegram number 21 of
January 7 8 p.m., regarding Axis propaganda and methods
MAR 1 1 1942
we might adopt to counteract it, I have the honor to
FILED
enclose a personal letter I have received from Cesar
Elejalde Chopitea, Director del Departamento Diplomático
of the Ministerio de Relaciones Exteriores del Perú,
inviting attention to the fact that despatches from
news service: in the United States frequently are so
811.91210
worded as to give the semblance of truth to the dis-
torted propaganda distributed by our enemies.
Mr. Elejalde sent me the two clippings which are
enclosed, with the observation that the one printing a
despatch from the North American Newspaper Alliance
"justifies the Japanese agression" and "makes Japanese
propaganda more successful", while the second contain-
ing a protest by a Bolivian newspaper against the
magazine
 Carbon copi

=== Page 337 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2-
e
magazine "Time" for its "depracatory attacks against
Latin merican countrie:" chows the need, as Mr. Elejalde
remarks, ror "an effective cor.trol of material for inter-
national publication, which material can be as dangerous
as acts of sabotage or substantial military reverses".
It occurs to me that many of the difficulties arise
from the fact that our newspapers, news services and
magazines are prepared for exclusive consumption in the
United States with unfortunate results when they fall
into the hands of Latins with different mental outlooks
and different emotional reactions. Most of the news
despatches and feature stories from services in the
United States appear to be mere translations of articles
originally prepared in English for American readers. All
of these should be carefully scrutinized and edited
before translation and they should be well sandwiched
with news and articles prepared especially for Latin
Americans.
Respectfully vours,
R. Henry Norweb

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure 1 to despatch no. 2592
dated January 13, 1942, from the
American Embassy, Lima, Peru.
Lima, January 8, 1942.
Excellency:
I am enclosing a clipping from today's "Comercio"
that corresponds to information from the North American
Newspaper lliance, prepared by Mr. George T. Hughes.
I cannot fin an explanation for the purpose of a
publication of this nature which in the analysis justi-
fies the Japanese agression. A publication of this
kind couldn't make Japanese propaganda more successful.
Also I am enclosing a clipping which appeared in a
Bolivian daily paper against "Time" for its deprecatory
attacks against Lat n American countries.
It is highly important that the United States
Government exercise an effective control of material
for international publicati n, which material can be as
dangerous and prejudicial as acts of sabotage or sub-
stantial military reverses.
4-
20210
With all our mutual feelings regarding the great
cause for which you are defending your country, we see
ith great regret that there still exists much incom-
prehension among certain well-known information
bureaus and newspaper writers in the United States.
With cordial greetings, I am,
Yours truly,

=== Page 343 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
whether such orders have been received and are being carried
out at the post office, and I shall endeavor to follow up
the matter with the view of having the orders carried out.
Both the news bulletin and the printed circular were
sent in plain envelopes with nothing to show the source.
Since the news bulletin consisted entirely of despatches
from Tokio, there seems to be little doubt that its origin
was Japanese. Dr. Bellido promised to point out to the
Japanese Legation that the publication of such bulletins
by diplomatic missions in Peru were contrary to law. I have
the feeling that the Japanese Legation is already well aware
of this and has probably arranged for the publication and
distribution to be undertaken by the Central Japanese
Society, an organization now under our close scrutinity.
The printed circular, urging Peru to take a stand for
neutrality at the Rio conference, is so worded as to make
it appear to be from a Peruvian source. However, the
phrases, and even the type employed, are very similar to
German propaganda which has come to my attention previously.
There is a way in which the source of these two publi-
cations might be established. I previously sent the De-
partment specimens of typewriters used at the German Legetion
and especially its Press Office. I am enclosing envelopes
in which the circulars described in foregoing paragraphs were
despatched and, if the Department sees no objection, it
would be appreciated if the typewriting on the envelopes
could be compared by experts with the samples previously
sent. If the comparison establishes that the source is
German, I can make good use of the knowledge.
Respectfully yours,
R.  Norwd
R. HenryNorweb

SUBJECT:
JAPANESE PROPAGANDA IN ARGENTINA
Strictly Confidential
894.
2
OF
1942
O
STATE
214
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
With reference to the Embassy's telegram no. 41
FEB 2 3 1942
of January 8, 1942, 3 p.m., I have the honor to report
FILED
that the Japanese propaganda has been intensified in
Argentina since the month of June 194l, when it became
apparent to the Japanese that they might be involved in
the present war. At that time Japan began to synohronize
the Japanese
PS/HM

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
the Japanese "cultural societies," in Latin America,
particularly those operating in Argentina, Brazil, Chile
and Mexico. At about the same time Japan began serious
propaganda effort throug the medium of radio, both short
wave broadcasts in Spanish from Japan and local programs,
using time purchased on the principal Latin American radio
stations.
The tempo of the Japanese propaganda effort has
been materially increased since December 7, when it was
brought into parallel operation with the efficient Nazi-
Fascist-Falangist propaganda machinery.
Japanese propaganda in Argentina seems to be
directed by Dr. Albino Pugnalin, former Argentine Minister
to Tokyo and at present understood to be employed by the
Japanese Embassy here, and Antonio J. de Silva Souza, an
interesting individual of Portuguese-Chinese parentage
born in British Malaya, former gun runner, at present
general passenger agent O.S.K. Steamship Line in Buenos
Aires and reputed to be the unofficial representative of
the Japenese Government in Argentina.
The direct and obvious propaganda here is concen-
trated in the Spanish section of the Japanese newspaper
El Argentin Djiji, in the monthly magazine Oriente y
Occidente (which is an exact duplicate in appearance of
the Readers Digest), in the Japanese Tourist Agency,
Chamber of Commerce folders and lectures to cultural groups.
Principal lecturer for the Japanese Cultural Institute,
a unit of the Mugeo Social Argentino, is George Yoshio
Shinya, who came here under the auspices of the Kokusai
Bunka Shinkokai, cultural propaganda arm of the Japanese
Foreign

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
Foreign Office. This latter has been in operation in
Argentina since 1936.
Japanese newsreels purporting to be a document
of Japnese military and naval victories over the Allies
in the Far Eastern theater of war, lave made their ap-
pearance within the last week in local theaters already
showing the official Nazi and Fascist propaganda news-
reels.
Since collaboration has been established between
the totalitarian "news" agencies, Transocean, Stefani
and Domei, all those newspapers and radio stations using
these services now disseminate the official Japanese
version of the progress of the war, and it is precisely
in this field that Japanese propaganda is most effective,
among the people of Argentina who are likely to be im-
pressed by superficial military successes.
The burden of political propaganda is carried by
Oriente y Occidente in which the viciously anti-United
States articles of Dr. Pugnalin are prominently featured.
In the January issue of Oriente y Occidente Dr. Pugnalin's
leading article, "Roosevelt y Castillo," predicts the
economic disintegration of the United States, character-
izing President Roosevelt as a demagogue and comparing
him unfavorably with Acting President Castillo of Argen-
tina, who is flattered in a subtle and effective way.
The same issue of the magazine, a copy of which
is enclosed with this despatch, includes an article by
Rear Admiral Tanetsugu pointing out the difficulties con-
fronting the fleets of the United States and Great Britain
in Far

=== Page 347 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
in Far Eastern wats, typical of the recent Japanese
military propaganda.
The Japanese hee not made the oropaganda headway
credited to the Nozis. Te argentine people are unable to
understand the mystic threa that runs through their
literature and their pamphleering, and it is particularly
hard for them to pras the Jaomese concent of the divinity
of the .ikado and the "Heaven-gu ded destiny" of the
Japanese race. The few Japan se i, Buenos Aires, somewhere
near 2,0o0, are, for the most part, ngaged in modest
cleaning, dyeing and pressing businesses, and it is diffi-
cult for the argentine people to understand them in the
same role of world conquest occupied in the public mind
by the Germans. The argentine aristocratic elass looks
upon the Japanese as an inferior race, and there is none
of that combined fear and adulation with which they looked,
for a time at least, on the Germans. For these and other
reasons, Japanese political and cultural propaganda is
not as effective as the energy of its presentation might
seem to warrant.
It would appear that Japanese propaganda here
can in no sense compete with United States propaganda so
long as we are able to present a fair and accurate picture
of ourselves and our activities to the Argentine people.
Respectfully yours
Enclosure as stated.
Norman
Armour

Vladivostok. (Reliebility undertain; dated 10 October 1941.)

UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Jom 21,1942
No. 3552
Bogotá, Colombia, January 21, 1942
Subject:
Hernful Effects of United Press and Havas
Despatches.
894/20210/1
891
5
8
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
PS/HM
sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's circular
telegram of January 6 4 a. m. and to my telegram in reply,
No. 19 of January 7, 1 p. m. reporting that there has been
little or no Japanese propaganda in Colombia except that con-
tained in United Press and Havas despatches to local newspapers.
Confidential File
A number of examples of both United Press and Havas des-
patches are enclosed for the information of the Department. It
will be noted that in most cases the United Press despatches
are marked "Tokyo via Vichy" and that the Havas despatches are
marked from Washington, Berlin or other cities. As an example
of a despatch from the Havas agency, there may be cited one
printed in EL SIGLo on December 30, 194l, bearing the Washington
date

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.99)
te line, Dec mber 29 snd published under the heading,
Donde está la flota de los stados Unidos" with the sub-
heading, "Antes de un a o no se enfronterá a la flota del
japón". A copy of this despatch is enclosed. Another Havas
despatch from Berlin dated January 19 was published in EL
SIGL0 on January 20 under the heading "Se anuncia para el
próximo diez de febrero la captura de la importante base de
Singapore por los japoneses". The Siglo he:ding does not
itself refer to the Havas despetch, which followed but to a
small news Item bearint a United Press date line of January
19 from Santiaco, Chile reading, "The correspondent of the
newspaper El Chileno in Japan reports that in the opinion of
Japanese military experts the fall of Singapore should take
place around the tenth of February." This same story is
followed by another United Press despatch from Tokyo by way
of Vichy dated January 20 in which it states that on January
17, a Japanese bombardment of Singapore destroyed 53 airplanes.
Yet another United Press despatch from Tokyo by way of
Vichy is one of January 19 which has been printed in a number
of papers under headlines such as that of EL ESPECTADOR which
read "Japón no tiene intenciones hostiles contra sur América"
and in EL TIElPo under the hesding "El japón envió un mensaje
a cuatro paises americanos". The story which followed was to
the effect that a spokesman for the Japanese Ministry of In-
formation had declared "Japan has absolutely no hostile
intentions toward the countries of South America" and that
Japan would do everything possible to avoid actions of war
unfavorable to Latin America and desired to resume its own
conmerce with those countries under normal conditions as soon
as possible.
Another Tokyo via Vichy despatch sent by the United Press
and published 1n E1 Tiempo on January 20, 1942 concludes "with
reference to the prisoners taken on Wake Island, the Domei
agency announced that they numbered 1,235, among whom were 30
officers, 423 non-commissioned officers and marines and 782
civilians. Among them was the Chief of the Air Force of the
island Putsam, who said that in the first moments of the Japanese
attack he lost eight planes and half his ground force." On
January 16 El Siglo published a U.P. Tokyo via Vichy despatch
of the previous day which said that the Japanese had sunk 36
ships in Pearl Harbor with a loss of at least 13,000 men. This
article continued, "It is added that Japanese losses to date are
only four destroyers, two submarines and two mine sweepers
totaling 8,000 tons as against the statements made by the
Americans that 28 war ships, including a cruiser of the Haruna
class, were sunk. It was also maid that the United States ad-
mitted the loss of naval units totaling 98,000 tons."
A report of the alleged sinking of an airplane carrier of
the Lexington type was carried in another U.P Tokyo via Vichy
article published in El Siglo on January 15.
From the enclosed clippings it will readily be seen that
these stories, whether from the United Press in Tokyo or from
the Havas agency, may be exceedingly harmful, the more so, since
the United Press is considered a reputable and unbiased news
agency. The publication of Japanese propaganda of this nature
under United Press date lines gives these stories a value to
which they are in no wise entitled and cannot but have an
unfortunato

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
unfortunate effect when published throughout Latin America.
In the case of the despatch from Washington mentioned
above, it woulc seen that the Department may possibly be
able to take some direct action against the Havas correspon-
dent while undoubtedly the United Press would agree not to
allow the use of their name in the dissemination of outright
propaganda stories from Tokyo.
Respectfully yours,
In triplicate
891
AED/ar

and Havas Despatches.

EL ESPECTADOR, JANUAHY 19
"Japón No Tiene Intenciones
Hostiles contra Suramérica"
TOKIO(Vía Vichy) enero 19. (U-
nited Press).—Un portavoz del mi-
nisterio de informaciones declaró
esta tarde que «el Japón no tiene,
en absoluto, intenciones hostiles
hacía los países suramericanos», y
expresó la esperanza de que los es-
tadistas de dichas naciones evita-
rían acceder a ciegas a las exigen-
cias norteamericanas para la rup-
tura de relaciones con Tokio.
El citado portavoz agregó que las
pacificas intenciones del Japón
quedaron de manifiesto con el re-
ciente mensaje que el ministro de
relaciones exteriores, Shigenori To-
gó, dirigió a los cancilleres de la
Agentina, el Brasil, Chile y el Pe-
rú.
El portavoz afirmó, además, que
el Imperio del Sol Naciente hace to-
do lo posible para evitar acciones
bélicas desfavorables con respectto a
la América latina y, además, desea
restaurar su propio comercio con
dichos países, en condtelones nor-
males lo antes posibles, reducién-
dose así al mínimo los peligros e-
conómicos que la zona suramerica-
na experimente en las condiciones
presentes.

On October 5, 1941, both Suhjects, together with the other Japanese
listed above, left Manzanillo by morning train, but instead of procceding
to Mexico City, they got off train at Guadalejara, where they were met by
the local Japaneso Con sul and other Japenese, two of vhom were reported to
be from San Frenciaco, Califomit, and the other Pron Canada.
On October 5, 194), the Jepanese Preighter "PERURAWA MARU" unexpedtedly
"put in" to the port of Acapulco and discharged one WAkAsUGI, travelling
on a diplomatic passport en routo to the United Statea and alleged to be a
special envoy to Washington from Tokyo.
Both subjects, together wit) UTIDA and WAKI, aro supposed to be connected
in some way with WAKAsJGI, al! of whom have transit visas and are en route
to Washington.
Both subjects errived in Ba boa. C. Z.. on Decenbor 3, 194l, by plane
from Mexico City.
Col. YosHlokA, age 38, carried diplomatic paseport "Ol7036, issued
November 24. at Mexico City. Re was scheduled to dopart from Cristobal,
C. Z., by plane December 5, destined for La Paz, Bolivia.
Major KANEKO, age 36, carried diplomatic passport O17039, issued
November 24, in Mexico.
On December 5, 1941, both subjects, with Lt. Col. UTIDA, arrived in
Lima, Peru, from Balboa by plane.
On the evening of December 17, KANEKO, and UTIDA, who also traveled
from Japan to the Americas in the same party, entertained at dinner at
the Hotel Maury, Lima, Peru, for the Socond Seotion of the Peruvian General
Staff. After the usual pleasantries in which they st ted they desired to
better Japanese relations with Peru end particularly with their army, Col.
UTIDA suggested that he and Major KANEKO were glad to offer their services
as instructors or to lecture at the Peruvian General Staff School on their
specialties, as Col. UTIDA was ar air expert and Major KANEkO was an
infantryman and a pacialist on landing operations.
It was further stated that they were both married but were traveling
alone and would like to meet some Peruvian girls in order to learn more
Spanish. They would like also to sit in at classes at the Military School.
They are now living at Calle Bueraventure Aguirre 399 with a Japanese
family.
Both subjects are reported to have claimed that Japan is certain to
dominate the Pacific and that the war may last ten years.
Lt. Col. YOSHIOKA was supposed to go to La Paz, Bolivia, as Japanese
M. A., but on December 26, he was still in Lima, Peru, attempting to obtain
a visa to go to La Pas, Bolivia. For some r eason, the Bolivian Government
held up permission for him to enter the country. It was reported that the
Japanese Government protested this action.
KANEKO and UTIDA appeared tc have been accredited as Military Attache
and Assistant Attache respectively for Peru and have made official calls
in the Ministry of War. They were not officially accepted by the Foreign
Minister until about January 15, 1942.
On January 20, 1942, YOSHIOKA, first resident Japanese Military
Attache in Bolivia, arrived at La Paz by rail from Peru.
There are many indications that the Japanose aro trying to place
agents on Pan American planes and on ships cruising out of U. S. ports
for South American ports in order to listen to the conversations of travelers,
and collect information on U. S. military and naval affairs.
Both of the subjects and Col. WAkI, formerly conneoted with the
Japanese Emoassy in Washington, D. C., who got out of the United States
opportunely, seem to be working on this phase of espionage. These three
officers are considered as dangerous spies.
Originally both subjects were supposed to be language officers assigned
to the Japanese Embassy in Washington, D. C., but it does not appear that
either one was in Washington for any length of time, if at all. Indications
are that both traveled in Central and South America on an espionage mission

for the Japanese Government.

Inquiry from the Mexicen Government desiring information on both subjeots.
Both are wanted by tho Mexican Covernment.
According to our informatica YOSHIOkA is still in Bolivia. The
Bolivian Government hes requested that Japanese Dip omats leave by February
26, but the matter is still pending due to protests of Japanese Diplomats
in Bolivia, and subject is probably still there.
Since Japanese Diplomats heve refused to leave Peru, KANeko and UTIDA
are presumably still there.
Lt. Col. YOSHIOKA also sppears under name "YOSIOKA" which is the new
official spelling in Japan, and is now used in Japar proper. However, the
spelling "YosHIokA" is the older form and is used generaliy outside of
Japan proper (the "C" sound is not found in Jepanese and is pronounced "SH").
Likewise the name "UTIDA" is the new official spelling in Japan, whereas
the clder form "UTcíDA" is still used generally outside of Japan proper.
In the name "MIYAzAWA", the "Z" is interchangeable with "S" both being
pronounced the same, a lthough "z" is preferable spelling. The name "TADAHuMI"
has appeared also as "TADAFUMI", the F" and "R" are the same sound in
Japanese, but "H" is preferable.
Lt. Col. Atsunori UTInA (UTCIIDA).

City.

for Lima. He carried diplomatic passport Ol7041, issued November 24 in
Mexico City - age 39. Subject was accompanied by Lt. Col. YOSIOKA, and
Major KANßkO, and arrived in Lima on December 5, 1941.
Subjeot, after making official calls on Ministry of War in Lima and
not being officially accepted by Foreign Minister, was finally officially
introduced by Japanese Legation as Military Attache.
UTIDA, with KANEKO, entertained at dinner for Second Section of
Peruvian General Staff on December 17 at Hotel Maury - (see summary of
KANEKO and YOSHIOKA.)
It is reported that Subjeot received more cables from Tokyo than
the Japanese Minister. He also received messages direot from TOJO, the
Japanese Foreign Minister. Thia faot was brought to the attention of an
official, who was quite concerned and said he was going to speak to the
President about the matter. He also said that it was strange that Japan
should suddenly send two Attaches to Lima when previously there had never
been any except one located in Brasil, who occasionally visited Lime.
Representations have been made to the Foreign Minister about these two
men (UTIDA and KANEKO) concerning the irregular manner of their arrival
without previous announoement and the suspioious nature of their activities.
Subjeot and KAnkkO are reported to have never previously served as
Attaches. They may be in Lima to direct sabotage activities. Subject has
claimed that Japan is certain to dominate the Pacific and that the war may
last ten years.
According to our information, Japanese Diplomats have refused to
leave Peru, and are probably still there.

Lt. Col. Tadahumi WAK.
Subject 1eft Japan September 5, 1941, on the TOAN MARU (AZUMA MARU)

194l, along with party which included: Lt. Col. Eitaro YOsHIOKA; Major
Rinakai KANEKO; Col. Atunori UTIDA, techmical advisor to Japanese Army;
Takasi ISII, M. A. Mexico City, Jiro MIYAZAWA, Secrctary to the Japanese

Reported as attached to Japanese Embassy in Washington, D. C. as
Assistant Military Attache, diplomatic visa issued on June 28, 194l, at
Tokyo. Subject's age 46.
However, Subject, together with above listed parties, left Manzanillo
on October 5, by train, and, instead of proceeding to Mexico City, they
got off train at Guadalajara, where they were met by local Japanese Consul
and other Japanese. On night of October 4, this group were allegedly
involved in an unsuccessful attempt to amuggle mercury on AZUMA MARU (see
summary on Lt. Col. YOSHIOKA and MaJor KANEKO.)
Subject was also believed to be among same group of Japanese officers
who were attempting to place agents on Pan American Airways planes and ships
oruising out of U. S. ports in order to collect information for the Japanese
Government.
Mexico City reported Subject was found to be one of two Japs. living
in a house at 388 Insurgentes, where a map of the Panama Canal was discovered
in his quarters. This map was marked with bombing targets.
Subject left suddenly on December 4, via PAa for Bogota, Colombia,
arriving in Balboe on December 5 and departing December 6 for Bogota,
travelling on Japanese diplomatic passport #017042.
Subject was previously reported to be working on plans to sabotage
Panama Canal.
If Subject is plotting sabotage of Panama Canal, he is now in a
position to constitute a greater danger. It would seem probable that WAKI
fled to Bogota in fear of the consequences immediately after the map was
stolen from his quarters in Mexico City.
When Subjeot moved out of the house at Insurgentes 388, Mexicc City,
it was noted that a large trunk was also moved out at the same time. A
trunk, believed to be the same one and belonging to Subject had been sent
to Avenida Insurgentes 480, Apt. 35, telephone 18-89-60. Investigation
established that this house was occupied by Col. Yoshiaki NISHI, the Military
Attache in Mexico, and his secretary MEDEA, both of whom were still there
December 17. It appeared thet WAKI could not take the large and heavy trunk
when he made his hurried trip by air, and therefore arranged for Col. NISI!
to guard the trunk at his quarters. Significance or value of the contents
of this trunk have not been established.
Aocording to latest information Japanese Diplomats in Colombia have
refused to leave that country and Subject is therefore probably still there.

MARU) and arrived in Mansanillo, Mexico, on September 30 or Ootober S, 194l.

(10) One message to "KOSHI, LIMA" dated December 8,

The above messages were submitted to a laboratory
examination and it was determined that cablegram no. 1 consisted
of a plain text Japanese message addressed to "Yamamoto Lapaz". A
translation of this message is as follows:
"The best way to prevent freezing of funds is to with-
draw deposits. Take care of this immediately. If it is necessary
to convert it to dollars, do so according to your telegram of today."
Message no. 2 is apparently transmitted in Japanese
Diplomatic Code, the address "Koshi" being used by the Japanese
Embassy or Iegation and the signature "Ishii" being the cable
signature used by the Rio de Janeiro Legation.
Message no. 3 is apparently transmitted in Japanese
Diplomatic Code. The cable address "Piyoji" is used by the Japanese
Consuls.
Messageno. 4-1l are addressed to "Koshi Lima" and are
signed "Irie" which signature is used by the La Paz Legation. All of
these messages are apparently transmitted in Japanese Diplomatic Code
or enciphered code. It is noted that messages 7-10-1l all contain
as the last group "Nerns". The exact meaning for this group is not
known.
None of these messages could be decoded in the Laboratory
inasmuch as the necessary code books are not available. Inasmuch as
the specimens submitted are photostatic copies, they are being retained
in the files of our Laboratory. However, if photostatic copies of
same are necessary to complete your files the Bureau will be glad
to forward copies of same.
Sincerely yours,

Attached is an interestin letter which has been
4.20210/1
addressed to the Coordinator of Inter-American Affairs,
conceming Japanese efforts to use anti-white race
propaganda in the other American republics.
This Japanese propaganda campaign seems to tie in
somewhat with the recent successful Japanese effort
to establish official representation at the Vatican.
In 1938, I believe, there arrived in Brazil a "friend-
ship mission" of Japanese Catholics. This mission was
9
sponsored by the Japanese government. It has recently
9
been brought to my attention by an American citizen
well informed in such matters (a former Foreign Service
offi cer) that the Japanese have long been paying attention
to Catholic relations in South America It is obvious
that, if they could overcome the natural distate for
themselves of South American Catholics, their race prop-
aganda would be greatly increased. My informant be-
lieved that the Japanese approach to the Holy See was
primarily for the purpose of facilitating propaganda
and subversive work in the Western Hemisphere. The
same informant called attention to the Japanese firm
of Hachiya and Company of Rio de Janeiro, stating that,
in his opinion, this firm was a center for apanese
Catholic activities and propaganda, connected with
Japanese agents in other parts of South America. This
firm is on the Black List. The possible extent to which
the Japanese effort in this direction has been carried
 is indicated by reports that they have made ap-
proaches, through Japanese Catholics (native) in southern
India. The same informant told me that in the Foreign
Office in Tokio, as advisor on Catholic matters, there
sits a certain Zeco (or Jocko ?) Amari. Amari, it appears,
lived for seventeen odd years in Chile and has a Cath-
olic wife.
MAY 1 3 1942

onn Fe

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
There has recently been a report that strong and
somewhat effectiv racist propaganda is being carried on
anong negroes employed in the C nal Zone. There have
been reports, also, of Axis-directed race propaganda---
no doubt imitating and possibly to some extent stemming
out of Communist race propaganda --- in British Carib-
bean possessions. This antedates the entrance of Japan
into the war, and appears to have been carried on some-
what under the supervision of Vicente Cardinales, or
Vincente Cardinali (who is now behind bars in Panama).
Evidently, it is much to the interest of Germany
and Italy to foster and assist Japanese propaganda among
Catholics in South America, lest the alliance with Japan
be harmful to German and Italian interests. This whole
situation, I believe, will bear watching.

JAPAIIEE ACTIVITIES ALONG THE WEST COAST OF SOUTH AMERICA
1.- it is well mown that certain cocial activit es have been brew
in anong the lower claspes in South America since World War I
part:culorly around the early 20's and that then they were ettributed and
traced to Communistic prope andn.
.- Although Mexico end Central Americn mre not altorether exempt
Pron the same damgere, no mention ie made here of thonse others
ac the Central Americen republice may be diccarded for the time bein:
and the Mexican situation has to be looked upon fron other angles on ac-
count of the different factors, circumstances, and possibilities pre-
vailing there.
3.- This underground influence has had marked or renewed activity
at certain periode when it has been said thet the focus or the
headquarters of foreign egents was in Mexico, or Ururuay, etc.
4.- thortly before the presont war, in the carly 3o's, preceeding
and/or coincidin with the arrival of the so-called Jewish exiles
from Germany end fugitives from Spain, the activities referred to recovered
new impetus end the blame was placed by some on the Nazis, while others
continued to attribute it to Red sources.
0
5.- Particularly was this undertow noticeable in the republics with
the larger percentage of Indian population--Bolivia, Peru, Ecuador.
C
6.- Evidence of a foreign influence has been found even among the
troops of both Ecuador and Peru during the recent armed conflict
between the two countries.
FLLD
7.- Now, the source is attributed to the Axis with no clear indicati
as to whether this has been the source before or that they are
using the already organized and stirred group that worked under influence
from Moscow.
8.- Reports from well versed persons, natives of the countries or
foreigners acquainted with their internal affairs, coincide
1
pretty closely in the belief that certain foreign propaganda is making
deep inroads into the lower masses (Indians and Mestizos) who are being
fed on the idea that this world situation is their opportunity to
wrest their rights from the foreigners and it is even expressed
TL
"from the whites," meaning the leading or soicalled aristocratic class
T
in each country.
L
①

=== Page 411 === (confidence 0.98)
Page 2
9. of cource, t nunt be borne in nind that in each country, or
somet men in ro tone  ench country, there are other fectors
that tend to prouote or deley that retiv ty.
10.- or instance, the epercucsione of the Chaco War in Bolivia,
the general economic situetion in Ecuador, the recent clash between
Ecuador and Peru, the Apriete movement in the latter, etc.
1l.- However, t he: been stated by a former Spanish diplomat in
Washin ton, who hes recontly nade e tour of the southern republics,
that he considere this situation must be watched very closely in Bolivia
and Poru.
12.- In Peru, he states, this is carried on by Japanese agents and
he finds reat similarity between the way this propaganda is
carried on in the three sections of Peru, where the Japanese element is
concentrated, with what has been done in recent year? and revealed by
the attitude of the native masses in Indo-China, Thai land, Malay, Dutch
East Indies, end, particulerly, Burme, where the masses hail the Japanese
invaders.
13.- Talking about this subject, another person, supposedly well
posted on the matter, said that fundamentally the information
is correct and added that he was informed that a Japanese article or
booklet entitled PARALLEL BETWEEN JAPANESE AND SPANISH CIVILIZATIONS
had been translated into Spanish end profusely circulated, particularly
along the west coast of South America end Mexico.
14.- Upon further investigation on this point, a third person has
assured the writer of the existence of such publication and that
it is a translation of an article which eppeared in the magazine,
CONTEMPORARY JAPAN.
15.- The first of the parties referred to considers that one of the
reasons why this movement entails a greater danger in Peru than
in the other countries is because the class distinction is more pronounced
there and that the leading social class distains and underrates the
possibilities of such a mass activity, thus leaving to the agents a larger
freedom of action than they would have if the danger were realized.
16.- He says that at Arequipa he was told in an intimate conversation
with Spanish and Peruvian friends how much they dreaded an upris-
ing of the Indians--stirred up by Japanese paid agents--as the aboriginies,
the "real owners of the land," against the "white" usurpers.

Mr. Duggan:
Some days ago, in a conversation which I had with
the Chinese Minister for Foreign Affairs, Dr. T. V.
Soong, I expressed the belief that Chinese diplomatic
and consular officials in the other American Republics
as well as leading members of the Chinese colonies in
those countries, could be of very great value in in-
894.20210/171
forming the governments in the countries where they
were living of Japanese subversive activities. I told
RA/sc/
him it was my understanding that this kind of assist-
ance by the Chinese had been uniquely helpful in West-
ern Mexico and in Peru.
The Minister replied that he felt this was a very
practical way in which the Chinese could be of help to
this country and that he would immediately send letters
of instruction covering these points to some of his
diplomatic officials in the other American Republics.
I told him we would be very glad to transmit these let-
ters for him through our pouches, since he has no secret
means of communication of his own.
JUN151942
701.
He has now sent me these letters, which I attach
herewith. Please draft covering instructions to our
own chiefs of mission in the countries mentioned ask-
ing them to hand these letters to the Chinese officials
and to discuss with the latter the best means of making
this step effective. I think the instructions should al-
S
so contain the request that they discuss the matter.with
the Foreign Ministers of the governments to which they
are accredited so that the way will be paved for a speedy
utilization by the respective governments of this Chinese
G
assistance.

I have the honor to refer to the Department's
telegram no. 583 of May 18, midnight, and to the
Embassy's despatch no. 3552 of January 21, 1942,
regarding the harmful effect of the continued publi-
cation in Colombian newspapers of United Press and
Havas despatches from Tokyo and other capitals.
Vhile the local correspondent of the United
Press claims that it is an impartial news agency
and is simply transmitting ofricial releases, many
of these stories are presented in such a way that
SEP.141942
19
they

=== Page 425 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
they cause reactions uniavorable to the interests
or the United States and the uther na:ions fighting
the Axis powers.
In accordance vith the request in the Depart-
ment's telegran no. 583, I am enclosing a number of
clippings of typical stories transritted by both the
United rress and Wavas. hile these clippings have
been taken largely fron. L1 Siglo, the only Bogotá
newspaper printing Havas despatches, the United Press
despatches have also often appeared in El Tiempo,
although generally with more truthful headlines.
Although El Siglo has in only one case commented
editorially on the international situation since the
entry of the United States into the war, it has con-
sistently published aistorted headlines, taking
advantage oi the injudicious selection of material
sent by the United ress. During recent weeks, owing
to the shortage of news print, 1 siglo has been forced
to reduce the size of the paper to six pages instead of
eight pa es, as formerly. This reduction has meant
the elimination of one entire page of international
news, and therefore apparently rewer Havas items have
appeared recently.
During a recent call on the I inister of war, the
 inister commented on the anomalous situation which
has arisen in this respect, when the (olombian Govern-
ment found it necessary to censor despatches contrary
to the interests of the United States although those
despatches had been passed by the censor in the
United States.
Respectfully yours,
Arthur Bliss Lane

No.
The Honorable,
Arthur Bliss Lane,
American Ambassador,
Bogotá.
Sir:
Reference 1s made to your despatoh no. 143or
May 22, 1942, with which were tranamitted a number of
clippings from Colombian newspapers containing despatohes
of the United Press and Havas from Berlin, Tokyo and
00
other cities; and to the Department's stridtly confiden-
6
tial 1nstruction no. 231, June 29, 1942.
The United Press articles included among the clip-
2
20210
pings transmitted to the Department with the despatch
of May 22 are evidently based upon the official Japanese
and German communiqués and news despatohes given over the
radio. Examination of the New York Times reveals that the
same stories, with two exceptions, appeared in that news-
paper and it is probable that other Amerjoan newspapers
carried the same articles. However, there is an important
difference in the treatment given to such artioles by loyal
2
American newspapers, as compared with that of El Siglo.
American newspapers, while printing the articles provided
from Axis countries, frequently prefacg them with editorial
notes calling attention to their origin, and rarely play

from the clippings provided by the Embassy, made a deri-

and German communiqués and news despatohes given over the
radio. Examination of the New York Times reveals that the
same stories, with two exceptions, appeared in that news-
paper and 1t 1s probable that other American newspapers
carried the same articles. However, there 1s an important
difference in the treatment given to such articles by loyal
American newspapers, as compared with that of El Siglo.
American newspapers, while printing the articles provided
from Axis countries, frequently preface them with editorial
notes calling attention to their origin, and rarely play
them up with important headlines. Ei Siglo has, to judge
from the clippings provided by the Embassy, made a deri-
nite effort to give prominence to these articles and to
display their claims in large headlines.
It is not the polioy of this Government, in censoring
news from other countries, to prohibit the printing of
claims made by enemy countries concerning military and
naval engagements. It is believed that the American public
should be able to read the claims of the enemy if they are

rumors of Axis successes which are so frequently and
adeptly oiroulated by local pro-Axis groups, and over the

It would appear, therefore, that the main problem,
insofar as the United Press artioles in Colombia are
concerned

ir:
I have the honor to refer to the mbassy's despatch
No. 3137 dated arch 19, 1942, file No. 820.02 entit1ed
"Investigation into the ictivities of the Japanese"
and to enclose a memorandum prepared by Mr. Emmerson
of the Auxiliary section describing a recent visit
to the Chancay Valley, made in company with Iir. Tu of
FILED
the Chinese Legation for the purpose of investigating
Japanese activities.
21942
The census of 1940 reports more than 10,000
Chinese in Peru. In general, this population parallels
PS/EG
the Japanese population in its distribution. It has
occurred to the Embassy that the enlistment of the
cooperetion of these Chinese in the investigation and
reporting of Japanese activities would be valuable.
With this in mind, the matter wes placed before the
Chinese

=== Page 439 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Chinese Legation in Lima and the Minister has ex-
presoed his complete willingness and desire to aid
us in every way possible.
A plan was outlined whereby a member of the
Auxiliary Section and a member of the Chinese Lega-
tion would visit representative Chinese in districts
where Japanese are found in substantial numbers. A
questionnaire was prepared (a copy of which is en-
closed) outlining the principal points on which
information was desired, and this was translated into
Chinese by the Legation. As the Minister had indi-
cated in previous interviews, Chinese residents are
willing enough to cooperate but lack initiative and
leadership. The questionnaire will serve to indicate
to the Chinese what observations may be of value.
A preliminery visit was made on May 19 by Mr. Wu
of the Chinese Legation and Mr. immerson to the
Chancay Valley north of Lima, a district containing
one of the important Japanese colonies in Peru. In
fact, the city of Huaral is popularly known as "Little
Tokyo". Calls were made at Chinese haciendas and upon
Chinese businessmen in Huaral, Chancay, and Huacho.
They showed eagerness to be of assistance and it is
believed that the visit was productive not only in
the information received at the time but in inspiring
the Chinese to keep on the alert in the future end to
report incidents and other informetion to us.
A second trip has been planned to the south for
the week beginning June 1, with investigations at
Cañete, Chincha, Nasca, Mollendo and Arequipa. The
Chinese inister has written letters to the heads of
Chinese associations in tiese cities requesting their
cooperation and he will also provide letters of intro-
duction to the various honorary consuls representing
Chinese interests in this district.
Respectfully yours,
Jefferson Patterson,
Charge d'Affaires, A. I.

=== Page 440 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch No. 379o, dated May 23,
1942, File No. 70l, from the American Embassy, Lima,
Peru.
IEIORANDUM
Intended to be the first in a series of reports
describing specific Japanese colonies in Peru, a
study is under preparation of the Japanese in the
Chancay Valley. Informat ;tined from the visit
to Huaral and Chancay on May 19, 1942, wil1 be
utilized in the writing of this report. The visit
vas furthermore productive in substantiating previous
information received by the Embessy in regard to Japanese
leaders, who have now been recommended to the Peru-
vian Government for exrulsion.
The following brief impressions ray be of inter-
est at this time:
1. Phe Chaneay Velley is an important
594.
cotton producing area. The most important citizen
is Nikunatsu OKADa, who has been decorated by
the mreror of Japan and who leases six cotton
plantations. wo other plantations cre operated
20223
by Je panese concerns and numerous Japanese are
either employees or yanecones (tenant farmers)
on the Peruvian haciendas.Residents estimate
the Japanese population of the Valley at from
4,000 to 5,000.
2. The Japanese schools (in Huaral, Hacienda
Esquivel and Huacho) are functioning and Japa-
nese have little difficulty in evading restric-
tions on their movements by means of bribes
to the police.
3. The Japanese have been extremely quiet
of late. They speak little and appear to be
worried about their situation in Peru at the
moment. Nevertheless, residents quoted familiar
expressions of confidence, such as "Japan will
light the whole world as the sun lights the heavens".
4. The Chinese think of the Japanese as
forming two distinct groups, the "real Japanese"
and the "Okinawans". At least two Chinese who
were interviewed stated that the Okinawans
disliked the Japanese because the latter treated
them as inferiors. Okinawans were quoted as
Confider
saying: "We are more like you (the Chinese)."
Others stated that the Okinawans were under the
thumb of their brothers from Naiohi (Japan Proper).
5. A Chinese in Chancay has observed parties
of Japanese fishing in the bay on Sundays.
6. The Japanese are said to be able to
replenish their stocks of merohandise.without
diffioulty.

=== Page 441 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
difficulty. Peruvians sell to them and buy
from them as usual. In Huacho, a Chinese
interviewed said: "All clothing and dry goods
stores are run by Japanese. Residents here must
either buy from them or make their purchases
in Lima."
7. The Chinese state that they have had no
relations with the Japanese since the beginning
of the Sino-Japanese conflict and therefore
are not in the best position to know of their
activities. However, without exception they
expressed their willingness to be of any
service possible. It is believed that under con-
tinued direction and prodding, the Chinese will
be of use in the investigation of Japanese
activities. The Chinese Minister is prepared
to take any official action we deem desirable
and the time of his secretary, Mr. Wu, is apparent-
ly at our disposal. This close cooperation
should prove of real value in the investigation
of the Japanese problem in Peru.

SUBJECT:

894.20210/18
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
JUN 2 2 1942
Washington.
DEPARTMENT
180
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's
strictly confidential instruction No. 800 of May 18,
1942, regarding the possibility of obtaining informa-
tion from Chinese diplomatic and consular officers in
JUN211942
the other American Republics, as well as from members
FILED
of the Chinese colonies in those countries in regard
to Japanese subversive activities.
I discussed this matter as it applies to Panamá
on May 29 with the Chinese Minister, Dr. Yorkson C. T.
SHEN, and delivered to him the sealed envelope enclosed
PS
with the Department's instruction.
/RIR
As the Department is aware, the Republic of Panamá
declared war upon Japan immediately after the Japanese
attack at Pearl Harbor, interned all Japanese nationals
in the Republio and transferred them to the United States
where they are now being held as Panamanian internees. It
is, therefore, the view of Dr. Shen, in which I concur,
that

=== Page 457 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
that with the removal of all Japanese from Panamá
there is little opportunity for him, or for Chinese
in Panama,to develop information of value in this field.
On the other hand, it would seem obvious that the physi-
cal removal of ali Jananese from the Republic does not
mean that subversive a ents in the pay of Japan have
ceased to operate in Jenamá. Cn the contrary there are
plenty of indications that espionage and the transmission
of information of nilitary value continues to take place
in Panana, and it seems reasonable to assume that some
of these activities are carried on by agents in the em-
ploy of Japan, as well as by Nazi, Fascist and Falange
agents. Dr. Shen said that he would of course be glad
to report to me any information which comes to his atten-
tion, or is communicuted to him by Chinese living in the
interior, regardin activities of persons suspected of
workine for Japan. He also stated that he was assisting
G-2 at Quarry Heights and the Censor's Office in the
Canal Zone by translating Japanese documents and corres-
pondence.
Dr. Shen has informed me that he will be leaving
Panamé in the immediate future under orders from his Gov-
ernment to return, via the United States, to Chungking.
A new Chinese Minister is to replace him in Panamá. In
this connection the Department will recall from previous
reports from the Embassy regarding the status of Chinese
nationals in Panama as affected by the Nationalization of
Commerce Law, that the Chinese colony in Panamá has blamed
Dr. Shen in large measure for the failure to relieve the
Chinese from the application of the provisions of this
Law. As I have reported to the Department, I believe that
Dr. Shen accomplished about all that was humanly possible
for his colony in connection with this matter, given the
exaggeratedly nationalistic spirit of the Arnulfo ARIAS
administration under which the law was enacted.
As of possible interest, on this question of the
status of the Chinese in the Republic of Panamá, the
following information was given me by Dr. Shen: He had
obtained about the middle of November 194l (as reported
to the Department), verbal assurances from the Foreign
Minister that Chinese married to Panamanian women who
placed their shops in the names of their wives would be
allowed to continue to work at the shops. This would have
permitted some 5o0 Chinese to continue effectively in con-
trol of their businesses. The assurances from the Foreign
Minister, however, were strictly confidential and the For-
eign Minister was unwilling to reduce them to writing.
The administration of the provisions of the Nationaliza-
tion of Commerce Law are in the hands of the Minister of
Commerce, Señor Ernesto FABREGA, a hold-over from the
Arnulfo Arias Government, and he has in practice effective-
ly nullified the verbal assurances of the Foreign Minister
by refusing in every case, after consideration of each ap-
plication, to issue the special permit required to allow
the Chinese to work in the stores owned by their wives.
Dr. Shen said that he still has this question under dis-
cussion with the Foreign Minister and the Minister of
Commerce

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Commerce, but he has little hope of accomplishing any-
thing heipful for his people.
Dr. Shen went on to say that of the some 3,000
Chinese in the Republic prior to the entrance into
force of the Nationalization of Commerce Law, there are
only about 1,000 still left in Panamá; over 800, the
wealthiest members of the colony, left Panamá after the
law became effective and proceeded to Hong Kong where
they were caught by the Japanese invasion; over 1,000
have been enabled to obtain work in the Canal Zone where
they still are.
In view of the situation as described at the begin-
ning of this despatch, which makes it seem unlikely that
the Chinese inister or members of his colony will develop
information of interest regarding Japanese subversive ac-
tivities in Panamá, I do not consider it necessary at this
time to discuss the matter with the Panamanian Minister
for Foreign Affairs. In the event the Chinese Minister
should ever uncover information of value, I shall of course
communicate it to the Panamanian Government.
Dr. Shen has today sent to me a sealed envelope ad-
dressed to the Chinese linister for Foreign Affairs, Dr.
T. V. SooNG, in reply to the letter enclosed with the De-
partment's instruction No. 8oo. He requests that this
letter be transmitted to Dr. Soong in Washington. The
letter is enclosed herewith.
Respectfully yours,
von
Edwin C. Wilson.
Enclosure:

Sir:

of June 5, 1942 (file No. 702/820.02), in which 1t was
stated that Dr. TCHOU Che-tsien, the Chinese Consul Gen-
eral in Guatemala, had offered to keep the Legation in-
formed of any Japanese subversive activities that might
come to his attention in this country or in El Salvador.
Dr. Tchou has been good enough to make available a
list and brief dossier on each adult in Guatemala of
Japanese nationality, or having a claim to such nation-
1/
ality.. The list is enclosed, in translation. It is as-
sumed that Dr. Tchou obtained his information principally
from the police, who are notoriously inept at spelling
foreign names. The Legation has therefore suggested
parenthetically corrections in some of the names to make
them more readily identifiable as Japanese. One name
has apparently been transliterated in the course of years
from something resembling "KAMITSU" to the Mayan name of
"CUMITZ".

With reference to the Départment's instruction No. 1469 of
May 18, 1942, I have the honor to report that on April 15, 1942,
the officer in charge of Proclaimed List matters inquired in-
formally of Dr. Dai, Chinese Chargé d'Affaires in Santiago,
whether he would have any objection to cooperating in such mat-
ters, and Dr. Dai replied that he would be glad to do so. Since
that time the Embassy has consulted Dr. Dai on several cases in-
volving Japanese. Dr. Dai has also been in touch with this
Mission for the past three months as regards Japanese subversive
activities.
